Yeah… mostly, yeah. It’s not just sadness about her being sick, though. I get stuck going back over everything — every appointment, every little change in how she was doing — and I start thinking, you know, if I’d been paying better attention maybe I would’ve caught something sooner.

So then I think, how did I not notice this? A better husband would’ve protected her somehow. That’s where my mind goes on the bad days.

I don’t know… I mean, it’s hard to separate it, because her health is woven into everything now. Like even making breakfast, I’m looking at the chair she sits in, or the cup she likes, and my mind goes there.

I don’t know… I mean, I worry, sure. Who doesn’t? There’s always something — the truck making that noise, whether I measured a cut wrong, bills showing up right when you think you’ve got a little breathing room.

But many different things… I’m not sure. My mind does wander all over the place sometimes. Like yesterday I went out to the garage to look for a screwdriver and ended up sorting through this old coffee can full of bent nails my dad would’ve thrown a fit about me keeping. He used to say, “A clean shop is a safe shop,” and mine’s never been either one.

Sorry… I’m getting off track. I guess I’m not sure how much of it is worry versus just thoughts bouncing around.

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is, I just don’t want to do the things that usually keep me going anymore. Like salsa nights — I used to love those, even if I wasn’t the best dancer. And church gatherings, cooking something for the neighbors… I used to do that without thinking.\n\nEven walking into my shop, you know, just to smell the wood and be around my tools — that used to settle me. Now I don’t even feel like doing that. It’s like the wanting part got shut off.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life shortly before this started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, something did. My wife’s health kind of suddenly went downhill, about two months ago, and everything in our routine changed after that.\n\nI mean, we had our ways of doing things — little things, like who made coffee, when I’d open the shop, what days we’d go by church — and then all of a sudden it wasn’t like that anymore. Funny how you don’t think a routine matters until it’s gone. But yeah, that’s when this whole change in me started, around then.\n\nDoctor: When you have a bad day emotionally, is it mostly because you’re thinking about your wife’s illness and how life has changed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yeah. It’s not just sadness about her being sick, though. I get stuck going back over everything — every appointment, every little change in how she was doing — and I start thinking, you know, if I’d been paying better attention maybe I would’ve caught something sooner.\n\nI know I’m not a doctor. I know that. I build cabinets and repair doorframes and all that… I’ve spent half my life noticing when a board is warped before anybody else sees it. So then I think, how did I not notice this? A better husband would’ve protected her somehow. That’s where my mind goes on the bad days.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not thinking about your wife’s illness, do you still feel down or unable to enjoy things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the thoughts about my wife make it worse, no question, but even when I’m not sitting there thinking on it, I still don’t really want to do anything.\n\nLike salsa night — I used to go even if I came home with sore knees, and I’d laugh about it. Or church gatherings, cooking a tray of something for the neighbors… those things used to fill me up. Even the shop, just walking in and smelling the wood, seeing the tools where I left them — that used to be enough to get me moving.\n\nNow I’ll stand there and it’s like… nothing catches. Like the wanting part of me isn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Aside from losing interest in things, have you been feeling sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, most days. Especially mornings. I wake up and before I even get out of bed there’s this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest.\n\nIt’s strange, because the house can look the same — same curtains, same dishes in the sink, same little creak in the hallway floor I’ve been meaning to fix for years — but it feels like the color’s gone out of it. Like everything’s dimmer.\n\nAnd I try not to show it too much in front of my wife. I’ll go make coffee or fuss with something in the kitchen just so I don’t break down where she can see me. But yeah… sad, down, whatever word fits. Most days.\n\nDoctor: If your wife’s health had not changed, do you think you would still be feeling this down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, it’s hard to separate it, because her health is woven into everything now. Like even making breakfast, I’m looking at the chair she sits in, or the cup she likes, and my mind goes there.\n\nBut… I think I’d still be down. Maybe not in the exact same way, but most mornings I wake up with this heavy, empty feeling in my chest before anything even happens. The house feels like the color’s gone out of it, you know? Dragging on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad week or regular stress. It’s settled in more than I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Before your wife became ill, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I’m trying to think. There were times I was low, sure. Life has a way of doing that to you. I’m not sure I’ve kept track of it that way. Things kind of blur together when you’re just trying to get through the day, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just your wife’s health?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I worry, sure. Who doesn’t? manifestation: Even after sitting down or getting a full night in bed, my body feels worn out like I have been framing a roof all day, and small chores like loading tools into the truck feel like too much.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being in my head, so I end up asking the younger guys to repeat things I should understand right away.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal manifestation: I took on extra restoration jobs, started mentoring the younger carpenters harder than usual, drew up business plans, and reached out to people I normally would have waited weeks to Yeah… I have. More than I probably want to admit.

I keep going back over every appointment, every time my wife seemed a little different, every little change in her health, and I think… why didn’t I catch that? It’s like when you’re building stairs and one measurement is off early, everything after that is off too. I keep thinking I missed the measurement. Like it’s on me somehow.

Sleep… I don’t know. I mean, it’s not like I’ve been keeping track of it.

Things kind of blur together when you’re just trying to get through the day, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just your wife’s health?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I worry, sure. My mind does wander all over the place sometimes. I guess I’m not sure how much of it is worry versus just thoughts bouncing around.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, was there ever a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to pin that down, but I honestly don’t know. I’ve had spells where I didn’t feel like doing much, where the shop just felt cold and the tools sat there, you know… and I’d tell myself I was just tired or work was slow.\n\nBut nearly every day for two weeks… before this? I can’t say for sure. I never marked it on a calendar or anything. Things sort of run together after a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.

There were times before where I got pretty low, or I stopped wanting to do things I usually cared about, but whether it was nearly every day for two full weeks… I can’t swear to that. That’s where my mind goes on the bad days.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not thinking about your wife’s illness, do you still feel down or unable to enjoy things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the thoughts about my wife make it worse, no question, but even when I’m not sitting there thinking on it, I still don’t really want to do anything.\n\nLike salsa night — I used to go even if I came home with sore knees, and I’d laugh about it. Or church gatherings, cooking a tray of something for the neighbors… those things used to fill me up. Even the shop, just walking in and smelling the wood, seeing the tools where I left them — that used to be enough to get me moving.\n\nNow I’ll stand there and it’s like… nothing catches. Like the wanting part of me isn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Aside from losing interest in things, have you been feeling sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, most days. Especially mornings. I wake up and before I even get out of bed there’s this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest.\n\nIt’s strange, because the house can look the same — same curtains, same dishes in the sink, same little creak in the hallway floor I’ve been meaning to fix for years — but it feels like the color’s gone out of it. Like everything’s dimmer.\n\nAnd I try not to show it too much in front of my wife. I’ll go make coffee or fuss with something in the kitchen just so I don’t break down where she can see me. But yeah… sad, down, whatever word fits. Most days.\n\nDoctor: If your wife’s health had not changed, do you think you would still be feeling this down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, it’s hard to separate it, because her health is woven into everything now. Like even making breakfast, I’m looking at the chair she sits in, or the cup she likes, and my mind goes there.\n\nBut… I think I’d still be down. Maybe not in the exact same way, but most mornings I wake up with this heavy, empty feeling in my chest before anything even happens. The house feels like the color’s gone out of it, you know? And I have to force myself to get moving, to not break down in front of her.\n\nI wish I could say it’s only because of what happened with her, but it feels bigger than that sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Before your wife became ill, had you been feeling down like this for years?\n\nPatient: No… not for years, not like this. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before — everybody does, right? Work slows down, bills pile up, you throw your back out lifting something you knew you shouldn’t have lifted… I can get gloomy.\n\nBut this… this has been more like, I don’t know, about six months now? Dragging on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad week or regular stress. It’s settled in more than I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Before your wife became ill, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I’m trying to think. There were times I was low, sure. Life has a way of doing that to you. I remember one winter when work was slow and I kept staring at the phone waiting for jobs to come in, and I’d go out to the garage just to move lumber around like that was going to fix anything.\n\nBut depressed like this… for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that. I’m not sure I’ve kept track of it that way. Things kind of blur together when you’re just trying to get through the day, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just your wife’s health?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I worry, sure. Who doesn’t? There’s always something — the truck making that noise, whether I measured a cut wrong, bills showing up right when you think you’ve got a little breathing room.\n\nBut many different things… I’m not sure. My mind does wander all over the place sometimes. Like yesterday I went out to the garage to look for a screwdriver and ended up sorting through this old coffee can full of bent nails my dad would’ve thrown a fit about me keeping. He used to say, “A clean shop is a safe shop,” and mine’s never been either one.\n\nSorry… I’m getting off track. I guess I’m not sure how much of it is worry versus just thoughts bouncing around.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, was there ever a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to pin that down, but I honestly don’t know. I’ve had spells where I didn’t feel like doing much, where the shop just felt cold and the tools sat there, you know… and I’d tell myself I was just tired or work was slow.\n\nBut nearly every day for two weeks… before this? I can’t say for sure. I never marked it on a calendar or anything. Things sort of run together after a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. More than I probably want to admit.\n\nI keep going back over every appointment, every time my wife seemed a little different, every little change in her health, and I think… why didn’t I catch that? A better husband would’ve noticed sooner, or pushed harder, or protected her somehow. I know that sounds— I don’t know, maybe it doesn’t make sense.\n\nIt’s like when you’re building stairs and one measurement is off early, everything after that is off too. I keep thinking I missed the measurement. Like it’s on me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I don’t know. I mean, it’s not like I’ve been keeping track of it.\n\nSome nights I’m in bed and I feel like I must’ve slept, because the clock’s moved, but it doesn’t feel like much. Other nights I’m just… thinking. Then I get annoyed at the sheets, or the room feels too quiet, and I start wondering if I should fix that loose hinge on the closet door because it catches every time you open it.\n\nBut has it changed? I’m not sure. Maybe. I couldn’t say exactly.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nThere were times before where I got pretty low, or I stopped wanting to do things I usually cared about, but whether it was nearly every day for two full weeks… I can’t swear to that. I wasn’t exactly taking notes. I’d just go to work, come home, maybe sit in the garage awhile pretending I was going to sharpen something or fix something, and then not really do it.\n\nBut that could’ve been a few days, or a week, or longer. Time gets funny when you’re just getting through it. So… I’m not saying no, I just can’t give you a solid yes either.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of what got me here, if I’m being honest.

It’s not just “sad,” exactly. It’s more like the things that usually keep me going just don’t pull me anymore. Salsa nights, for one—I used to like that, even if I wasn’t the best dancer in the room. Like salsa nights — I used to love those, even if I wasn’t the best dancer. And church gatherings, cooking something for the neighbors… I used to do that without thinking.\n\nEven walking into my shop, you know, just to smell the wood and be around my tools — that used to settle me. Now I don’t even feel like doing that. It’s like the wanting part got shut off.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life shortly before this started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, something did. My wife’s health kind of suddenly went downhill, about two months ago, and everything in our routine changed after that.\n\nI mean, we had our ways of doing things — little things, like who made coffee, when I’d open the shop, what days we’d go by church — and then all of a sudden it wasn’t like that anymore. Funny how you don’t think a routine matters until it’s gone. But yeah, that’s when this whole change in me started, around then.\n\nDoctor: When you have a bad day emotionally, is it mostly because you’re thinking about your wife’s illness and how life has changed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yeah. It’s not just sadness about her being sick, though. I get stuck going back over everything — every appointment, every little change in how she was doing — and I start thinking, you know, if I’d been paying better attention maybe I would’ve caught something sooner.\n\nI know I’m not a doctor. I know that. I build cabinets and repair doorframes and all that… I’ve spent half my life noticing when a board is warped before anybody else sees it. So then I think, how did I not notice this? A better husband would’ve protected her somehow. That’s where my mind goes on the bad days.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not thinking about your wife’s illness, do you still feel down or unable to enjoy things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the thoughts about my wife make it worse, no question, but even when I’m not sitting there thinking on it, I still don’t really want to do anything.\n\nLike salsa night — I used to go even if I came home with sore knees, and I’d laugh about it. Or church gatherings, cooking a tray of something for the neighbors… those things used to fill me up. Even the shop, just walking in and smelling the wood, seeing the tools where I left them — that used to be enough to get me moving.\n\nNow I’ll stand there and it’s like… nothing catches. Like the wanting part of me isn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Aside from losing interest in things, have you been feeling sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, most days. Especially mornings. I wake up and before I even get out of bed there’s this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest.\n\nIt’s strange, because the house can look the same — same curtains, same dishes in the sink, same little creak in the hallway floor I’ve been meaning to fix for years — but it feels like the color’s gone out of it. Like everything’s dimmer.\n\nAnd I try not to show it too much in front of my wife. I’ll go make coffee or fuss with something in the kitchen just so I don’t break down where she can see me. But yeah… sad, down, whatever word fits. Most days.\n\nDoctor: If your wife’s health had not changed, do you think you would still be feeling this down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, it’s hard to separate it, because her health is woven into everything now. Like even making breakfast, I’m looking at the chair she sits in, or the cup she likes, and my mind goes there.\n\nBut… I think I’d still be down. Maybe not in the exact same way, but most mornings I wake up with this heavy, empty feeling in my chest before anything even happens. The house feels like the color’s gone out of it, you know? And I have to force myself to get moving, to not break down in front of her.\n\nI wish I could say it’s only because of what happened with her, but it feels bigger than that sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Before your wife became ill, had you been feeling down like this for years?\n\nPatient: No… not for years, not like this. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before — everybody does, right? Work slows down, bills pile up, you throw your back out lifting something you knew you shouldn’t have lifted… I can get gloomy.\n\nBut this… this has been more like, I don’t know, about six months now? Dragging on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad week or regular stress. It’s settled in more than I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Before your wife became ill, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I’m trying to think. There were times I was low, sure. Life has a way of doing that to you. I remember one winter when work was slow and I kept staring at the phone waiting for jobs to come in, and I’d go out to the garage just to move lumber around like that was going to fix anything.\n\nBut depressed like this… for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that. I’m not sure I’ve kept track of it that way. Things kind of blur together when you’re just trying to get through the day, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just your wife’s health?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I worry, sure. Who doesn’t? There’s always something — the truck making that noise, whether I measured a cut wrong, bills showing up right when you think you’ve got a little breathing room.\n\nBut many different things… I’m not sure. My mind does wander all over the place sometimes. Like yesterday I went out to the garage to look for a screwdriver and ended up sorting through this old coffee can full of bent nails my dad would’ve thrown a fit about me keeping. He used to say, “A clean shop is a safe shop,” and mine’s never been either one.\n\nSorry… I’m getting off track. I guess I’m not sure how much of it is worry versus just thoughts bouncing around.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, was there ever a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to pin that down, but I honestly don’t know. I’ve had spells where I didn’t feel like doing much, where the shop just felt cold and the tools sat there, you know… and I’d tell myself I was just tired or work was slow.\n\nBut nearly every day for two weeks… before this? I can’t say for sure. I never marked it on a calendar or anything. Things sort of run together after a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. More than I probably want to admit.\n\nI keep going back over every appointment, every time my wife seemed a little different, every little change in her health, and I think… why didn’t I catch that? A better husband would’ve noticed sooner, or pushed harder, or protected her somehow. I know that sounds— I don’t know, maybe it doesn’t make sense.\n\nIt’s like when you’re building stairs and one measurement is off early, everything after that is off too. I keep thinking I missed the measurement. Like it’s on me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I don’t know. I mean, it’s not like I’ve been keeping track of it.\n\nSome nights I’m in bed and I feel like I must’ve slept, because the clock’s moved, but it doesn’t feel like much. Other nights I’m just… thinking. Then I get annoyed at the sheets, or the room feels too quiet, and I start wondering if I should fix that loose hinge on the closet door because it catches every time you open it.\n\nBut has it changed? I’m not sure. Maybe. I couldn’t say exactly.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nThere were times before where I got pretty low, or I stopped wanting to do things I usually cared about, but whether it was nearly every day for two full weeks… I can’t swear to that. I wasn’t exactly taking notes. I’d just go to work, come home, maybe sit in the garage awhile pretending I was going to sharpen something or fix something, and then not really do it.\n\nBut that could’ve been a few days, or a week, or longer. Time gets funny when you’re just getting through it. So… I’m not saying no, I just can’t give you a solid yes either.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, before this, I don’t know if I could put a calendar around it and say “that was exactly two weeks,” you know?\n\nBut the feeling itself—yeah. Most mornings I’d wake up and there’d be this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest. Like the whole house had the color drained out of it. And I’d have to sort of… brace myself before getting up, because I didn’t want my wife to see me fall apart.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but morning light used to tell me what needed fixing—trim, paint, whatever—and then it just started feeling like another thing I had to get through. So… yes, I think there were stretches like that, even if I can’t pin down the exact length.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of what got me here, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not just “sad,” exactly. It’s more like the things that usually keep me going just don’t pull me anymore. Salsa nights, for one—I used to like that, even if I wasn’t the best dancer in the room. Church gatherings too, cooking something for the neighbors… I used to make this big pot of beans sometimes, and people would come by after, you know, just talking in the driveway.\n\nAnd my shop… that’s the one that bothers me. I used to walk in there just to smell the wood, even if I wasn’t building anything. Now I don’t even want to go in. So, yeah, I guess there have been times like that, nearly every day, where I just stopped wanting any of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

So… yes, I think there were stretches like that, even if I can’t pin down the exact length.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of what got me here, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not just “sad,” exactly. It’s more like the things that usually keep me going just don’t pull me anymore. Salsa nights, for one—I used to like that, even if I wasn’t the best dancer in the room. Church gatherings too, cooking something for the neighbors… I used to make this big pot of beans sometimes, and people would come by after, you know, just talking in the driveway.\n\nAnd my shop… that’s the one that bothers me. I used to walk in there just to smell the wood, even if I wasn’t building anything. Now I don’t even want to go in. So, yeah, I guess there have been times like that, nearly every day, where I just stopped wanting any of it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo your low mood and loss of interest ever come on even when nothing about your wife’s illness is on your mind?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… sometimes it’s not like I’m actively thinking about her being sick.

I’ll wake up and it’s just there already, before I’ve even had a proper thought about anything. This heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like the house has gone flat somehow, like all the color’s been drained out of it.

And then I’m lying there trying to get myself together before she notices—because I don’t want to break down in front of her. I’ll start thinking, well, maybe I should fix that loose hinge or check the back steps, but it’s not really about the hinge. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.9, F32.9, F43.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is, I just don’t want to do the things that usually keep me going anymore. Like salsa nights — I used to love those, even if I wasn’t the best dancer. And church gatherings, cooking something for the neighbors… I used to do that without thinking.\n\nEven walking into my shop, you know, just to smell the wood and be around my tools — that used to settle me. Now I don’t even feel like doing that. It’s like the wanting part got shut off.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life shortly before this started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, something did. My wife’s health kind of suddenly went downhill, about two months ago, and everything in our routine changed after that.\n\nI mean, we had our ways of doing things — little things, like who made coffee, when I’d open the shop, what days we’d go by church — and then all of a sudden it wasn’t like that anymore. Funny how you don’t think a routine matters until it’s gone. But yeah, that’s when this whole change in me started, around then.\n\nDoctor: When you have a bad day emotionally, is it mostly because you’re thinking about your wife’s illness and how life has changed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yeah. It’s not just sadness about her being sick, though. I get stuck going back over everything — every appointment, every little change in how she was doing — and I start thinking, you know, if I’d been paying better attention maybe I would’ve caught something sooner.\n\nI know I’m not a doctor. I know that. I build cabinets and repair doorframes and all that… I’ve spent half my life noticing when a board is warped before anybody else sees it. So then I think, how did I not notice this? A better husband would’ve protected her somehow. That’s where my mind goes on the bad days.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not thinking about your wife’s illness, do you still feel down or unable to enjoy things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the thoughts about my wife make it worse, no question, but even when I’m not sitting there thinking on it, I still don’t really want to do anything.\n\nLike salsa night — I used to go even if I came home with sore knees, and I’d laugh about it. Or church gatherings, cooking a tray of something for the neighbors… those things used to fill me up. Even the shop, just walking in and smelling the wood, seeing the tools where I left them — that used to be enough to get me moving.\n\nNow I’ll stand there and it’s like… nothing catches. Like the wanting part of me isn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Aside from losing interest in things, have you been feeling sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, most days. Especially mornings. I wake up and before I even get out of bed there’s this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest.\n\nIt’s strange, because the house can look the same — same curtains, same dishes in the sink, same little creak in the hallway floor I’ve been meaning to fix for years — but it feels like the color’s gone out of it. Like everything’s dimmer.\n\nAnd I try not to show it too much in front of my wife. I’ll go make coffee or fuss with something in the kitchen just so I don’t break down where she can see me. But yeah… sad, down, whatever word fits. Most days.\n\nDoctor: If your wife’s health had not changed, do you think you would still be feeling this down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, it’s hard to separate it, because her health is woven into everything now. Like even making breakfast, I’m looking at the chair she sits in, or the cup she likes, and my mind goes there.\n\nBut… I think I’d still be down. Maybe not in the exact same way, but most mornings I wake up with this heavy, empty feeling in my chest before anything even happens. The house feels like the color’s gone out of it, you know? And I have to force myself to get moving, to not break down in front of her.\n\nI wish I could say it’s only because of what happened with her, but it feels bigger than that sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Before your wife became ill, had you been feeling down like this for years?\n\nPatient: No… not for years, not like this. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before — everybody does, right? Work slows down, bills pile up, you throw your back out lifting something you knew you shouldn’t have lifted… I can get gloomy.\n\nBut this… this has been more like, I don’t know, about six months now? Dragging on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad week or regular stress. It’s settled in more than I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Before your wife became ill, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I’m trying to think. There were times I was low, sure. Life has a way of doing that to you. I remember one winter when work was slow and I kept staring at the phone waiting for jobs to come in, and I’d go out to the garage just to move lumber around like that was going to fix anything.\n\nBut depressed like this… for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that. I’m not sure I’ve kept track of it that way. Things kind of blur together when you’re just trying to get through the day, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just your wife’s health?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I worry, sure. Who doesn’t? There’s always something — the truck making that noise, whether I measured a cut wrong, bills showing up right when you think you’ve got a little breathing room.\n\nBut many different things… I’m not sure. My mind does wander all over the place sometimes. Like yesterday I went out to the garage to look for a screwdriver and ended up sorting through this old coffee can full of bent nails my dad would’ve thrown a fit about me keeping. He used to say, “A clean shop is a safe shop,” and mine’s never been either one.\n\nSorry… I’m getting off track. I guess I’m not sure how much of it is worry versus just thoughts bouncing around.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, was there ever a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to pin that down, but I honestly don’t know. I’ve had spells where I didn’t feel like doing much, where the shop just felt cold and the tools sat there, you know… and I’d tell myself I was just tired or work was slow.\n\nBut nearly every day for two weeks… before this? I can’t say for sure. I never marked it on a calendar or anything. Things sort of run together after a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. More than I probably want to admit.\n\nI keep going back over every appointment, every time my wife seemed a little different, every little change in her health, and I think… why didn’t I catch that? A better husband would’ve noticed sooner, or pushed harder, or protected her somehow. I know that sounds— I don’t know, maybe it doesn’t make sense.\n\nIt’s like when you’re building stairs and one measurement is off early, everything after that is off too. I keep thinking I missed the measurement. Like it’s on me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I don’t know. I mean, it’s not like I’ve been keeping track of it.\n\nSome nights I’m in bed and I feel like I must’ve slept, because the clock’s moved, but it doesn’t feel like much. Other nights I’m just… thinking. Then I get annoyed at the sheets, or the room feels too quiet, and I start wondering if I should fix that loose hinge on the closet door because it catches every time you open it.\n\nBut has it changed? I’m not sure. Maybe. I couldn’t say exactly.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nThere were times before where I got pretty low, or I stopped wanting to do things I usually cared about, but whether it was nearly every day for two full weeks… I can’t swear to that. I wasn’t exactly taking notes. I’d just go to work, come home, maybe sit in the garage awhile pretending I was going to sharpen something or fix something, and then not really do it.\n\nBut that could’ve been a few days, or a week, or longer. Time gets funny when you’re just getting through it. So… I’m not saying no, I just can’t give you a solid yes either.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, before this, I don’t know if I could put a calendar around it and say “that was exactly two weeks,” you know?\n\nBut the feeling itself—yeah. I wasn’t exactly taking notes. I’d just go to work, come home, maybe sit in the garage awhile pretending I was going to sharpen something or fix something, and then not really do it.\n\nBut that could’ve been a few days, or a week, or longer. So… I’m not saying no, I just can’t give you a solid yes either.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, before this, I don’t know if I could put a calendar around it and say “that was exactly two weeks,” you know?\n\nBut the feeling itself—yeah. Most mornings I’d wake up and there’d be this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest. Like the whole house had the color drained out of it. I’m not great with exact dates, but it felt like it went on long enough that it wasn’t just a rough patch.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore this current period, was there another separate time in your life when you felt this same kind of depression for at least two weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 55 / carpenter / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like the color has gone out of the house and I have to force myself not to break down in front of my wife.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I think so. It’s hard to separate them out cleanly, because after a while all those mornings kind of run together.

But there was another stretch where most mornings I’d wake up and it was already there—this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like the house had lost all its color. I’d just lie there a bit, listening to the pipes or the birds outside, thinking I should get up and do something useful… and instead I’d be trying not to fall apart before my wife saw me.

So, yeah. It wasn’t just a day or two. It went on long enough that I’d say it was at least a couple weeks.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.9, F32.9, F43.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is, I just don’t want to do the things that usually keep me going anymore. Like salsa nights — I used to love those, even if I wasn’t the best dancer. And church gatherings, cooking something for the neighbors… I used to do that without thinking.\n\nEven walking into my shop, you know, just to smell the wood and be around my tools — that used to settle me. Now I don’t even feel like doing that. It’s like the wanting part got shut off.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life shortly before this started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, something did. My wife’s health kind of suddenly went downhill, about two months ago, and everything in our routine changed after that.\n\nI mean, we had our ways of doing things — little things, like who made coffee, when I’d open the shop, what days we’d go by church — and then all of a sudden it wasn’t like that anymore. Funny how you don’t think a routine matters until it’s gone. But yeah, that’s when this whole change in me started, around then.\n\nDoctor: When you have a bad day emotionally, is it mostly because you’re thinking about your wife’s illness and how life has changed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yeah. It’s not just sadness about her being sick, though. I get stuck going back over everything — every appointment, every little change in how she was doing — and I start thinking, you know, if I’d been paying better attention maybe I would’ve caught something sooner.\n\nI know I’m not a doctor. I know that. I build cabinets and repair doorframes and all that… I’ve spent half my life noticing when a board is warped before anybody else sees it. So then I think, how did I not notice this? A better husband would’ve protected her somehow. That’s where my mind goes on the bad days.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not thinking about your wife’s illness, do you still feel down or unable to enjoy things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the thoughts about my wife make it worse, no question, but even when I’m not sitting there thinking on it, I still don’t really want to do anything.\n\nLike salsa night — I used to go even if I came home with sore knees, and I’d laugh about it. Or church gatherings, cooking a tray of something for the neighbors… those things used to fill me up. Even the shop, just walking in and smelling the wood, seeing the tools where I left them — that used to be enough to get me moving.\n\nNow I’ll stand there and it’s like… nothing catches. Like the wanting part of me isn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Aside from losing interest in things, have you been feeling sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, most days. Especially mornings. I wake up and before I even get out of bed there’s this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest.\n\nIt’s strange, because the house can look the same — same curtains, same dishes in the sink, same little creak in the hallway floor I’ve been meaning to fix for years — but it feels like the color’s gone out of it. Like everything’s dimmer.\n\nAnd I try not to show it too much in front of my wife. I’ll go make coffee or fuss with something in the kitchen just so I don’t break down where she can see me. But yeah… sad, down, whatever word fits. Most days.\n\nDoctor: If your wife’s health had not changed, do you think you would still be feeling this down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, it’s hard to separate it, because her health is woven into everything now. Like even making breakfast, I’m looking at the chair she sits in, or the cup she likes, and my mind goes there.\n\nBut… I think I’d still be down. Maybe not in the exact same way, but most mornings I wake up with this heavy, empty feeling in my chest before anything even happens. The house feels like the color’s gone out of it, you know? And I have to force myself to get moving, to not break down in front of her.\n\nI wish I could say it’s only because of what happened with her, but it feels bigger than that sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Before your wife became ill, had you been feeling down like this for years?\n\nPatient: No… not for years, not like this. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before — everybody does, right? Work slows down, bills pile up, you throw your back out lifting something you knew you shouldn’t have lifted… I can get gloomy.\n\nBut this… this has been more like, I don’t know, about six months now? Dragging on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad week or regular stress. It’s settled in more than I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Before your wife became ill, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I’m trying to think. There were times I was low, sure. Life has a way of doing that to you. I remember one winter when work was slow and I kept staring at the phone waiting for jobs to come in, and I’d go out to the garage just to move lumber around like that was going to fix anything.\n\nBut depressed like this… for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that. I’m not sure I’ve kept track of it that way. Things kind of blur together when you’re just trying to get through the day, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just your wife’s health?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I worry, sure. Who doesn’t? There’s always something — the truck making that noise, whether I measured a cut wrong, bills showing up right when you think you’ve got a little breathing room.\n\nBut many different things… I’m not sure. My mind does wander all over the place sometimes. Like yesterday I went out to the garage to look for a screwdriver and ended up sorting through this old coffee can full of bent nails my dad would’ve thrown a fit about me keeping. He used to say, “A clean shop is a safe shop,” and mine’s never been either one.\n\nSorry… I’m getting off track. I guess I’m not sure how much of it is worry versus just thoughts bouncing around.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, was there ever a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to pin that down, but I honestly don’t know. I’ve had spells where I didn’t feel like doing much, where the shop just felt cold and the tools sat there, you know… and I’d tell myself I was just tired or work was slow.\n\nBut nearly every day for two weeks… before this? I can’t say for sure. I never marked it on a calendar or anything. Things sort of run together after a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. More than I probably want to admit.\n\nI keep going back over every appointment, every time my wife seemed a little different, every little change in her health, and I think… why didn’t I catch that? A better husband would’ve noticed sooner, or pushed harder, or protected her somehow. I know that sounds— I don’t know, maybe it doesn’t make sense.\n\nIt’s like when you’re building stairs and one measurement is off early, everything after that is off too. I keep thinking I missed the measurement. Like it’s on me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I don’t know. I mean, it’s not like I’ve been keeping track of it.\n\nSome nights I’m in bed and I feel like I must’ve slept, because the clock’s moved, but it doesn’t feel like much. Other nights I’m just… thinking. Then I get annoyed at the sheets, or the room feels too quiet, and I start wondering if I should fix that loose hinge on the closet door because it catches every time you open it.\n\nBut has it changed? I’m not sure. Maybe. I couldn’t say exactly.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nThere were times before where I got pretty low, or I stopped wanting to do things I usually cared about, but whether it was nearly every day for two full weeks… I can’t swear to that. I wasn’t exactly taking notes. I’d just go to work, come home, maybe sit in the garage awhile pretending I was going to sharpen something or fix something, and then not really do it.\n\nBut that could’ve been a few days, or a week, or longer. Time gets funny when you’re just getting through it. So… I’m not saying no, I just can’t give you a solid yes either.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, before this, I don’t know if I could put a calendar around it and say “that was exactly two weeks,” you know?\n\nBut the feeling itself—yeah. Most mornings I’d wake up and there’d be this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest. Like the whole house had the color drained out of it. And I’d have to sort of… brace myself before getting up, because I didn’t want my wife to see me fall apart.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but morning light used to tell me what needed fixing—trim, paint, whatever—and then it just started feeling like another thing I had to get through. So… yes, I think there were stretches like that, even if I can’t pin down the exact length.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of what got me here, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not just “sad,” exactly. It’s more like the things that usually keep me going just don’t pull me anymore. Salsa nights, for one—I used to like that, even if I wasn’t the best dancer in the room. Church gatherings too, cooking something for the neighbors… I used to make this big pot of beans sometimes, and people would come by after, you know, just talking in the driveway.\n\nAnd my shop… that’s the one that bothers me. I used to walk in there just to smell the wood, even if I wasn’t building anything. Now I don’t even want to go in. So, yeah, I guess there have been times like that, nearly every day, where I just stopped wanting any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do your low mood and loss of interest ever come on even when nothing about your wife’s illness is on your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it’s not like I’m actively thinking about her being sick.\n\nI’ll wake up and it’s just there already, before I’ve even had a proper thought about anything. This heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like the house has gone flat somehow, like all the color’s been drained out of it.\n\nAnd then I’m lying there trying to get myself together before she notices—because I don’t want to break down in front of her. I’ll start thinking, well, maybe I should fix that loose hinge or check the back steps, but it’s not really about the hinge. It’s just… the morning hits me that way sometimes, whether I’ve been thinking about her illness or not.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a separate time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… separate from now, yes, I think there was. I know I keep circling around it, but the mornings are what I remember most.\n\nI’d wake up and before I even had my feet on the floor, there’d be this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like someone had taken all the color out of the house. And I’d just lie there trying to pull myself together, because I didn’t want my wife to see me break down.\n\nIt wasn’t just one bad morning, either. It was… most mornings, day after day. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

In the shop, I’ve had to hand off parts of jobs I’d normally keep an eye on myself. I’ve always been pretty particular about that—measure twice, make sure the trim lines up right, the little stuff people don’t notice unless it’s wrong. But lately I just… I can’t seem to stay on top of all of it.

And it’s not just work, I guess. I’ve kind of pulled back from people too—church, friends, the neighbors. My wife noticed, and even someone from the parish got worried enough to check in about whether I was safe. That was… embarrassing. But yeah, it’s been affecting things.

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is, I just don’t want to do the things that usually keep me going anymore. Like salsa nights — I used to love those, even if I wasn’t the best dancer. Now I don’t even feel like doing that. It’s like the wanting part got shut off.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life shortly before this started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, something did. My wife’s health kind of suddenly went downhill, about two months ago, and everything in our routine changed after that.\n\nI mean, we had our ways of doing things — little things, like who made coffee, when I’d open the shop, what days we’d go by church — and then all of a sudden it wasn’t like that anymore. Funny how you don’t think a routine matters until it’s gone. But yeah, that’s when this whole change in me started, around then.\n\nDoctor: When you have a bad day emotionally, is it mostly because you’re thinking about your wife’s illness and how life has changed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yeah. It’s not just sadness about her being sick, though. I get stuck going back over everything — every appointment, every little change in how she was doing — and I start thinking, you know, if I’d been paying better attention maybe I would’ve caught something sooner.\n\nI know I’m not a doctor. I know that. I build cabinets and repair doorframes and all that… I’ve spent half my life noticing when a board is warped before anybody else sees it. So then I think, how did I not notice this? I mean, the thoughts about my wife make it worse, no question, but even when I’m not sitting there thinking on it, I still don’t really want to do anything.\n\nLike salsa night — I used to go even if I came home with sore knees, and I’d laugh about it. Or church gatherings, cooking a tray of something for the neighbors… those things used to fill me up. Even the shop, just walking in and smelling the wood, seeing the tools where I left them — that used to be enough to get me moving.\n\nNow I’ll stand there and it’s like… nothing catches. Like the wanting part of me isn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Aside from losing interest in things, have you been feeling sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, most days. Especially mornings. I wake up and before I even get out of bed there’s this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest.\n\nIt’s strange, because the house can look the same — same curtains, same dishes in the sink, same little creak in the hallway floor I’ve been meaning to fix for years — but it feels like the color’s gone out of it. Like everything’s dimmer.\n\nAnd I try not to show it too much in front of my wife. I’ll go make coffee or fuss with something in the kitchen just so I don’t break down where she can see me. Maybe not in the exact same way, but most mornings I wake up with this heavy, empty feeling in my chest before anything even happens. The house feels like the color’s gone out of it, you know? And I have to force myself to get moving, to not break down in front of her.\n\nI wish I could say it’s only because of what happened with her, but it feels bigger than that sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Before your wife became ill, had you been feeling down like this for years?\n\nPatient: No… not for years, not like this. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before — everybody does, right? Dragging on long enough that it doesn’t feel like just a bad week or regular stress. It’s settled in more than I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Before your wife became ill, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I’m trying to think. There were times I was low, sure. Life has a way of doing that to you. I remember one winter when work was slow and I kept staring at the phone waiting for jobs to come in, and I’d go out to the garage just to move lumber around like that was going to fix anything.\n\nBut depressed like this… for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that. I’m not sure I’ve kept track of it that way. Things kind of blur together when you’re just trying to get through the day, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, not just your wife’s health?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I worry, sure. Who doesn’t? There’s always something — the truck making that noise, whether I measured a cut wrong, bills showing up right when you think you’ve got a little breathing room.\n\nBut many different things… I’m not sure. My mind does wander all over the place sometimes. Like yesterday I went out to the garage to look for a screwdriver and ended up sorting through this old coffee can full of bent nails my dad would’ve thrown a fit about me keeping. He used to say, “A clean shop is a safe shop,” and mine’s never been either one.\n\nSorry… I’m getting off track. I guess I’m not sure how much of it is worry versus just thoughts bouncing around.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, was there ever a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to pin that down, but I honestly don’t know. I’ve had spells where I didn’t feel like doing much, where the shop just felt cold and the tools sat there, you know… and I’d tell myself I was just tired or work was slow.\n\nBut nearly every day for two weeks… before this? I can’t say for sure. I never marked it on a calendar or anything. Things sort of run together after a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. More than I probably want to admit.\n\nI keep going back over every appointment, every time my wife seemed a little different, every little change in her health, and I think… why didn’t I catch that? A better husband would’ve noticed sooner, or pushed harder, or protected her somehow. I know that sounds— I don’t know, maybe it doesn’t make sense.\n\nIt’s like when you’re building stairs and one measurement is off early, everything after that is off too. I keep thinking I missed the measurement. Like it’s on me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I don’t know. I mean, it’s not like I’ve been keeping track of it.\n\nSome nights I’m in bed and I feel like I must’ve slept, because the clock’s moved, but it doesn’t feel like much. Other nights I’m just… thinking. Then I get annoyed at the sheets, or the room feels too quiet, and I start wondering if I should fix that loose hinge on the closet door because it catches every time you open it.\n\nBut has it changed? I’m not sure. Maybe. I couldn’t say exactly.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nThere were times before where I got pretty low, or I stopped wanting to do things I usually cared about, but whether it was nearly every day for two full weeks… I can’t swear to that. I wasn’t exactly taking notes. I’d just go to work, come home, maybe sit in the garage awhile pretending I was going to sharpen something or fix something, and then not really do it.\n\nBut that could’ve been a few days, or a week, or longer. Time gets funny when you’re just getting through it. So… I’m not saying no, I just can’t give you a solid yes either.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, before this, I don’t know if I could put a calendar around it and say “that was exactly two weeks,” you know?\n\nBut the feeling itself—yeah. Most mornings I’d wake up and there’d be this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest. Like the whole house had the color drained out of it. And I’d have to sort of… brace myself before getting up, because I didn’t want my wife to see me fall apart.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but morning light used to tell me what needed fixing—trim, paint, whatever—and then it just started feeling like another thing I had to get through. So… yes, I think there were stretches like that, even if I can’t pin down the exact length.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of what got me here, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not just “sad,” exactly. It’s more like the things that usually keep me going just don’t pull me anymore. This heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like the house has gone flat somehow, like all the color’s been drained out of it.\n\nAnd then I’m lying there trying to get myself together before she notices—because I don’t want to break down in front of her. I know I keep circling around it, but the mornings are what I remember most.\n\nI’d wake up and before I even had my feet on the floor, there’d be this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like someone had taken all the color out of the house. And I’d just lie there trying to pull myself together, because I didn’t want my wife to see me break down.\n\nIt wasn’t just one bad morning, either. It was… most mornings, day after day. I’m not great with exact dates, but it felt like it went on long enough that it wasn’t just a rough patch.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, was there another separate time in your life when you felt this same kind of depression for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It’s hard to separate them out cleanly, because after a while all those mornings kind of run together.\n\nBut there was another stretch where most mornings I’d wake up and it was already there—this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like the house had lost all its color. I’d just lie there a bit, listening to the pipes or the birds outside, thinking I should get up and do something useful… and instead I’d be trying not to fall apart before my wife saw me.\n\nSo, yeah. It wasn’t just a day or two. It went on long enough that I’d say it was at least a couple weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. It’s hard to separate them out cleanly, because after a while all those mornings kind of run together.\n\nBut there was another stretch where most mornings I’d wake up and it was already there—this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like the house had lost all its color. I’d just lie there a bit, listening to the pipes or the birds outside, thinking I should get up and do something useful… and instead I’d be trying not to fall apart before my wife saw me.\n\nSo, yeah. It wasn’t just a day or two. It went on long enough that I’d say it was at least a couple weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I eat, but I don’t know that I’ve been paying close attention to it.\n\nSome days I’ll stand in the kitchen and open the fridge and forget what I was even looking for, then notice that little catch on the door still sticks—been meaning to plane it down, just haven’t. My wife will ask if I want something and I’ll say “yeah, sure,” but I couldn’t tell you if I’m hungrier or less hungry than usual.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Nothing I’ve clearly noticed, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to keep up with your work in the shop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. More than I like to admit.\n\nIn the shop, I’ve had to hand off parts of jobs I’d normally keep an eye on myself. I’ve always been pretty particular about that—measure twice, make sure the trim lines up right, the little stuff people don’t notice unless it’s wrong. But lately I just… I can’t seem to stay on top of all of it.\n\nAnd it’s not just work, I guess. I’ve kind of pulled back from people too—church, friends, the neighbors. My wife noticed, and even someone from the parish got worried enough to check in about whether I was safe. That was… embarrassing. But yeah, it’s been affecting things."
